--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion is simply `--nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 4` (always Stop), with score 9929.27. This makes perfect sense: before the switch time, robots should be stationary (score=0 per step), and after the switch, they need to move. But a pure Stop FSM scores 0 before switch and 20*remaining_steps after switch.

Wait, let me reconsider. The score is MINIMISED. Before switch: stationary robots add 1/step each. After switch: moving robots add 1/step each.

So the optimal strategy is:
- **Before switch**: all robots MOVE (score 0 per step)
- **After switch**: all robots STOP (score 0 per step)

The problem is robots don't know when the switch happens. The green wall lights up at switch time - robots can perceive this with C=7 (color perception).

So the ideal FSM:
- State 0: Move (Exploration) - before switch, adds 0 to score
- State 1: Stop - after switch, adds 0 to score
- Transition: When green color (color 2) is perceived → switch from Move to Stop

The champion (always Stop) scores ~9929 because it's stationary before switch (bad) but still stationary after (good). With 400-600 step switch window and 1200 total steps:
- Expected switch at ~500 steps
- Always Stop: ~500 steps * 20 robots moving... wait, no. Stop = stationary = bad before switch, good after switch.
- Always Stop score: ~500 * 20 = 10000 (before switch, all 20 stationary) + 0 (after switch) ≈ 10000. That matches 9929!

Always Move score: 0 (before switch) + ~700 * 20 = 14000 (after switch). That's worse.

The key insight: perceive GREEN (color 2) to switch from Move to Stop.

- State 0: Exploration (moving) - before switch
- State 1: Stop - after switch
- Transition 0→1: C=7, l=2 (green), high probability (e.g., 0.9)
- Transition 1→0: should be very low or none (don't want to go back to moving)

The champion must survive. Let me generate 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2-8: Move→Stop on green detection (core strategy), various parameters
9-14: Exploration variants

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 4

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion uses `GoToColor` (B=8) with color 2 (green) in state 0, transitioning to Stop when green is perceived (C=7, l=2). This is brilliant: it moves toward the green wall when it appears, and once close enough to perceive it reliably, switches to Stop. Score: 7596.41.

The core pattern is clear:
- **State 0**: Move somehow (GoToColor green, Exploration, etc.)
- **Transition 0→1**: On perceiving green (C=7, l=2, high probability)
- **State 1**: Stop
- **Transition 1→0**: Very low probability random (to avoid getting stuck)

**What works:**
- GoToColor(green) + stop-on-green: 7596 (best)
- Exploration + stop-on-green: ~9200-9900 range
- GoToColor is better because it actively seeks the signal

**What to try:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Vary the green perception probability (0.9, 0.95, 1.0) for GoToColor
3. Try GoToColor with different speeds/approaches
4. Try 3-state: explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop
5. Try higher perception probability for stop transition
6. Make stop transition probability closer to 1.0 when seeing green
7. Try very small back-transition probability from Stop
8. Try 3-state with GoToColor as intermediate

The key insight: GoToColor green moves toward the wall, making the green signal stronger and easier to detect. The transition probability of 0.9 seems good. Let me try varying:
- p0x0 from 0.8 to 1.0 for the green detection
- The stop-to-move transition probability (currently 0.005)
- Adding a second transition from state 0 (e.g., random small prob to ensure movement)

Also: the 3-state machine ranked 2nd used Exploration→GoToColor(green)→Stop with score 9197. But GoToColor directly is better.

Let me focus on exploitation of the champion topology with variations, plus some 3-state exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The champion (6432.78) is a 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), transitions to State 1 when green perceived (p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop, can return to State 0 when green perceived (p=0.01, l=2) or randomly (p=0.002)
- State 2: GoToColor(green), transitions to State 1 when green perceived (p=0.9)

Wait - State 1 transitions to State 1? No - destination indices are among OTHER states. With 3 states, from state 1, destinations are [0,2]. So n1x0 dest=1 means state 2, and n1x1 dest=0 means state 0.

So: Stop → GoToColor(green) [state 2] with p=0.002 (random) OR stop → GoToColor(green) [state 0] when green seen (p=0.01). State 2 → Stop when green seen. This creates a loop where if green disappears from view, robots go back to seeking it.

The 2-state version with extra random transition (rank 2, 6649) is also strong.

**Key insights:**
1. The 3-state champion adds a "re-seek green" fallback which helps when robots lose sight of green
2. The small p=0.002 random return to seeking is crucial
3. The green perception probability of 0.9 seems optimal
4. Having two GoToColor states (0 and 2) both seeking green and both leading to Stop is elegant

**Exploitation plan:**
- Vary p values slightly around champion
- Try different back-transition probabilities
- Try 4-state versions adding more structure
- Try making the stop→seek transition triggered by NOT seeing green

**Exploration:**
- 4-state: GoToColor → Stop → GoToColor(fallback) → Stop
- Try having Stop use C=4 (neighbors below threshold) to trigger re-movement

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The champion (6154.85) has:
- State 0: GoToColor(green), → State 1 when green perceived (p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop, → State 2 with p=0.002 (random), OR → State 0 when green perceived (p=0.02)
- State 2: GoToColor(green), → State 1 when green perceived (p=0.9)

Key observation: The back-transition from Stop when green is seen (p=0.02) improved over p=0.01. This makes sense - if a robot in Stop state sees green, it should go TOWARD it (not away), which helps robots cluster near the green wall and stay stopped.

Wait, actually: if you see green and you're stopped, shouldn't you stay stopped? But the transition sends you to GoToColor(green), which makes you move toward it. This is counterintuitive but might help robots that aren't near the wall to find it.

Actually the logic is: when in Stop and you see green (p=0.02), go to GoToColor - but GoToColor immediately transitions back to Stop if green is perceived (p=0.9). So robots near green oscillate: Stop → GoToColor → Stop rapidly. That's actually fine as long as they net stop most of the time.

The bigger benefit of p=0.02 vs p=0.01 is that robots that stop before the switch time and THEN see green after the switch will more quickly re-engage with the green signal and stay near it.

**Parameter tuning focus:**
- Try p1x1 (stop→seek-green when green seen) at 0.02, 0.03, 0.05, 0.015
- Try p1x0 (random stop→seek) at 0.001, 0.002, 0.003
- Try perception probability 0.9 vs 0.85 vs 0.95 for all transitions
- The 4-state version tied for 2nd - worth exploring with p1x1=0.02

**Exploration:**
- Try a 4-state with two Stop states and two GoToColor states
- Try having the GoToColor state also check for neighbors to decide movement
- Try p1x1=0.0 (no green-triggered return) to see if it helps

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

The champion (6154.85) still holds. The best new machine scored 6207.88 (p1x1=0.03). The scores are very tightly clustered between 6207-6422 for the top 12, with a big gap to 13th (7310) and 14th (9136).

Key observations:
1. p1x1 (green-triggered return from Stop to GoToColor): 0.03 > 0.02 > 0.015, but 0.05 is slightly worse. Sweet spot seems around 0.02-0.03.
2. Adding random transition from GoToColor state (n2x1, p=0.001) slightly helps (rank 2)
3. The 4-state version with extra GoToColor+Stop pair also performs well
4. p0x0=0.9 (green detection probability) seems optimal vs 0.88 or 0.92
5. Neighbor-count transitions (C=3) as extra conditions are VERY harmful (rank 14)
6. Having NO green-triggered return from Stop (rank 13, 7310) is much worse

**What to try:**
- p1x1 around 0.025, 0.03, 0.035 (fine-tuning around 0.02-0.03)
- p1x0 (random return): try 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002
- Combined: add small random transition from state 2 (GoToColor) to state 0 (also GoToColor, effectively a reset)
- Try p=0.03 with p1x0=0.001 
- Try 4-state with p1x1=0.03
- Try different green detection probabilities: 0.87, 0.89

The pattern is converging. Let me do fine-grained exploitation around the best configurations plus some moderate exploration.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

The champion (6154.85) still holds. The best new scores are ~6193 (tied). The scores are extremely tight - all within 6193-6466.

Key findings:
1. **p1x1 = 0.03** with **n2x1 (random GoToColor→GoToColor, p=0.001)** gives 6192.91 (best this round)
2. The 4-state mirror of the 3-state also gives 6192.91
3. **p1x1 = 0.03** alone (no n2x1) gives 6256 - slightly worse
4. **p1x1 = 0.04** gives 6250 - close
5. **p0x0 = 0.89** gives 6233 - slightly better than 0.9 in some configs
6. The champion uses p1x1=0.02, but 0.03 consistently outperforms it in new runs (stochastic noise matters)

The winning pattern: `GoToColor(green) → [on green: Stop] → [on green: GoToColor2, random: GoToColor2] → GoToColor2 → [on green: Stop, random→GoToColor1]`

Wait, let me re-examine: in rank 1, state 2 has TWO transitions: n2x0 (→ state 1=Stop when green, p=0.9) AND n2x1 (→ state 0=GoToColor when random, p=0.001). This means GoToColor state 2 randomly jumps to GoToColor state 0, providing variety.

**Strategy for Round 7:**
- Keep champion
- Exploit: p1x1 around 0.03, add random transition from state 2 (p=0.001)
- Try combining: p1x1=0.03 + n2x1=0.001 + p0x0 variations (0.88, 0.89, 0.90)
- Try p1x1=0.035 
- Try p2x1=0.0005 or 0.002 (different random from GoToColor)
- Try 4-state with p1x1=0.03 and n2x1=0.001
- Some exploration with different GoToColor parameters

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (6154.85) still holds. All new machines score between 6223-6398. The scores are incredibly tight.

Key observations:
1. **Best this round**: p1x1=0.025, n2x1=0.001 (6223.51) - slightly better than p1x1=0.03 with n2x1=0.001
2. p1x1=0.035 with n2x1=0.001 gives 6289 - slightly worse
3. p2x1=0.001 consistently better than 0.0005 or 0.002 for the random GoToColor→GoToColor transition
4. p0x0=0.89 gives 6310 - slightly worse than 0.9
5. The champion uses p1x1=0.02 without n2x1, and it still beats everything new
6. Adding n2x1=0.001 seems to help slightly vs not having it

**Pattern emerging:**
- Best configurations cluster around p1x1=0.02-0.03 and n2x1=0.001
- p0x0=0.9 is the sweet spot
- p1x0=0.002 seems optimal for random Stop→GoToColor

The stochasticity of runs means the champion (6154) might just be a lucky run. The true optimum is likely around 6200-6300.

**Strategy for Round 8:**
- Keep champion
- Try combining the best features: p1x1=0.025, n2x1=0.001 (best this round)
- Fine-tune: p1x1=0.022, 0.024, 0.026, 0.028
- Try p2x1=0.0008, 0.0012 (fine-tune the random GoToColor reset)
- Try 4-state version with p1x1=0.025, n2x1=0.001
- Try adding a small random transition from state 0 (GoToColor) to state 2
- Some broader exploration: different architecture

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

New champion (6134.90)! The winning machine has state 2 with 3 transitions:
- n2x0: → Stop when green seen (p=0.9)
- n2x1: → GoToColor0 randomly (p=0.001)  
- n2x2: → Stop when green seen AGAIN (p=0.9, same condition!)

Wait - two transitions with the same condition type C=7 (color perception, l=2) from state 2? This is interesting - having two green-detection transitions both going to Stop (state 1) effectively doubles the probability of transitioning to Stop when green is seen, since both fire independently.

Actually destination indices: from state 2, other states are [0,1]. So n2x0 dest=1 (Stop), n2x2 dest=1 (Stop again). Both transitions go to Stop when green is perceived. Having two such transitions raises effective probability. Combined with n2x1 (random to state 0=GoToColor).

This effectively means: from GoToColor2, transition to Stop with probability ~(1-(1-0.9)*(1-0.9)) = 0.99 when green is seen, plus occasionally reset to GoToColor0.

**Key insight**: Doubling the green-detection transition significantly helps! The robot is almost certain to stop when it sees green.

**Strategy for Round 9:**
- Keep champion
- Try 4 transitions from state 2 (adding more green detections or randoms)
- Try similar doubling in state 0 (GoToColor0)
- Try different p values for the doubled green detection (0.85+0.85 vs 0.9+0.9)
- Try p1x0=0.0015 (which also did well at rank 2)
- Try combining p1x0=0.0015 with n2x2 trick
- Try adding n0x1 (second green detection) to state 0

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9:**

The champion (6134.90) still holds. The best new machine scored 6197.45 using p2x0=p2x2=0.95 (instead of 0.9). The scores are very tight (6197-6344).

Key observations:
1. **p2x0=p2x2=0.95** (both green detections at 0.95) gives 6197 - best new result
2. The base champion with p2x0=p2x2=0.9 gives ~6262 when re-run (stochastic)
3. **p2x1=0.0005** gives 6247 - slightly worse than 0.001
4. **p1x0=0.0015** gives 6244 - close
5. 4 green detection transitions (rank 10, 6287) is slightly worse than 3
6. Doubling the green detection in state 0 (n0x1) gives 6287 - not helpful there
7. Doubling in state 1 (n1x2 for green-triggered return) gives 6344 - worse

**Final round strategy:**
This is the last round. I should:
1. Keep the champion
2. Try p2x0=p2x2=0.95 with variations (p1x1=0.02, 0.025, 0.03; p1x0=0.001, 0.0015, 0.002; p2x1=0.001)
3. Try p2x0=p2x2=0.92, 0.93
4. Try combinations of the best-performing parameters
5. A few broader explorations

The champion structure is clearly converged. Last round = maximize exploitation with just enough variation.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
